## Run of 2026-08-08 (late) — THIRD pass, on 1.21.0 bundles (skill fork, component hooks, background delivery)

Both bundles rebuilt from a clean tree at `550b41e31` (the 1.21.0 close-out),
so they contain R69b, R86, R87, R88, R89. App started with no renderer errors
and no exception on the new component-hook path.

| case | result |
| :-- | :-- |
| `AGT-1` | **PASS** 10/10 — `/subtask` inherits history, a named subagent does not, no `/fork`. `control-parent-leaked-into-prompt: false` confirmed |
| `AGT-2` | **PASS** 14/14 — fork panel row, transcript, follow-up reaches the fork (`PINEAPPLE-ACK`), second fork stopped, finished fork dismissed. This exercises the background CHILD lifecycle that R86 delivers on |
| `AGT-3` | **PASS** 7/7 — a fork's permission prompt reaches the user and answering unblocks it |
| `AGT-4` | **6/7** — output style reaches a fork and NOT a named subagent, live, both directions. The one open check is an unimplemented half of the CASE (it rewinds but never has a fork+subagent edit a file first) and the memory half needs a marker the harness must not plant |
| `AGT-5` | **5/5 driven, blocked on OBSERVABILITY** — session at low, spawn, mid-session change to high, second spawn all pass; the effort a spawn actually used is exposed nowhere, confirmed AGAIN against the dev log this run (grep found no child-start effort line). Contract is unit-tested; this is a gap in what can be observed |
| `AGT-6` | **2/5, and the passing ones are the point** — `the UserPromptSubmit hook's stdout reached the model` PASS; `the compact reminder is not delivered repeatedly` PASS. The compaction half fails on setup (`/compact` → "Nothing to compact, session too small"), not product — the case needs to build real context first |
| `AGT-7`, `AGT-8` | BLOCKED on fixtures (a plugin with a sensitive `userConfig`; a deployed managed policy) — neither exists on this host |

**None of the failures is a product regression from the 1.21.0 units** — every one
is an unimplemented case half, a missing fixture, or an observability gap, each
labelled as such.

### The new units, and how each is verified

- **R69b (`context: fork` skill)** — the fork/child MACHINERY it rides is proven
  live by AGT-1/2/3. The skill-specific path (a `context: fork` skill spawning its
  named-subagent child, history-isolated) is proven by unit tests with a real
  `spawnChildSession` and a parent-only canary. Driving it live needs a session
  whose PROJECT is the fixture directory — the harness creates sessions in the
  operator's own workspace, so the `~/nessy-cdp-fixtures/.nessy-agent/skills/cdp-fork`
  skill is out of scope for it. Recorded, not driven.
- **R86 (background result → model)** — the background child lifecycle is exercised
  live by AGT-2; the "completion notification reaches the model on a later turn"
  path is proven by unit tests driving the real `PiEventAdapter` → real
  `SessionManager`. No dedicated live case.
- **R87 (component skill hooks)** — needs a fixture-project session for the same
  reason as R69b; unit-tested for the lifetime property (fires while active, gone
  after finish) with an orchestrator control. The app loaded the new dispatch path
  without error.
- **R88 (plugin Update fetches the new version)** — needs a plugin whose catalog
  advertises a newer version, which no marketplace on this host offers. Unit-tested
  including the honest-failure paths.
- **R89 (argument injection guard)** — a guard; proven by unit tests with the
  audit's own vector, not drivable as a happy-path UI case.

## Run of 2026-08-08 — SECOND pass, on bundles that actually contain the wave

The first pass (below) ran against bundles built BEFORE R64/R69/R73/R75 landed.
Both bundles were rebuilt from the final tree and the wave re-run.

| case | result |
| :-- | :-- |
| `AGT-1` | **PASS** 10/10 — and it incidentally proved a hook: the control subagent answered with `NESSY-CDP-PROMPT-HOOK-77A1`, i.e. the `UserPromptSubmit` hook's stdout had reached the model's context |
| `AGT-2` | **FAIL 12/14, and the case says why itself** — the fork FINISHED before the follow-up was sent (`child-status-at-followup: completed`), so the follow-up had nothing live to reach. Its own detail reads "it was already terminal, so this measures nothing and the task needs to be longer". A harness timing dependency, not a regression: it passed on the earlier run only because that fork happened to still be running. **The case needs a longer child task**, not a product fix |
| `AGT-3` | **PASS** 7/7 — a fork's permission prompt reaches the user, is attributed to the fork, and answering it unblocks the child |
| `AGT-4` | **6/7** — the output-style half passes live in both directions again (fork inherits, named subagent does not). The remaining check is an unimplemented half OF THE CASE: it rewinds the transcript but never has a fork and a subagent edit a file first. Its memory half needs `NESSY_CDP_MEMORY_MARKER`, deliberately not driven because the harness must not append to the operator's own memory files |
| `AGT-5` | **BLOCKED → DRIVEN, then blocked on OBSERVABILITY.** Unblocked with the existing `cdp-high-effort` fixture: all five driving checks pass (session at `low`, spawn, mid-session change to `high`, second spawn). The case cannot score it because **the effort a spawn actually used is exposed nowhere** — not on the Pi runtime snapshot the renderer reads, and the dev log does not carry it either (checked). The contract itself is covered by a unit test (`restrictions.thinkingLevel`), so this is an observability gap, not a product gap |
| `AGT-6` | **2/5, and the passing one is the point.** `the UserPromptSubmit hook's stdout reached the model` — **PASS, live**. The compaction half failed on setup, not product: `/compact` returned `Nothing to compact (session too small)`, so the `SessionStart(compact)` hook had no compaction to fire on. The no-duplicate-reminder check passed. **The case needs to build real context before compacting** |
| `AGT-7`, `AGT-8` | still BLOCKED — a plugin declaring a `userConfig` with a sensitive option, and a deployed managed policy. Neither fixture exists on this host |

### Verified outside the app, because the adapter is the honest place for it

Two of this wave's nine new cases are about IMPORT, which is decided before any
UI is involved. Both formats were driven through their real adapters on real
bundles built for this purpose (`~/nessy-cdp-fixtures/`):

- **Claude bundle** (`.claude-plugin/plugin.json` + `hooks/hooks.json`): imports
  `ok`, its `UserPromptSubmit` and `SessionStart` hooks arrive, and `Setup` is
  refused as `plugin.deferred.event_setup_no_launch_mode`.
- **Codex bundle** (`.codex-plugin/plugin.json` + skill + hooks): imports `ok`,
  the skill AND the hook arrive, `Setup` is refused with the same code — and the
  Codex path additionally emits a **`warning`** diagnostic naming it, so the
  author is told rather than left guessing.
- **Every skill on this host validates**: 8 of 8 with zero errors and zero
  warnings, including the bundled `code-review`, `skill-creator`,
  `plugin-creator` and `loop`.
- A skill's `!` placeholder parses as `echo …` with the **backticks already
  stripped** — the fix that makes an `allowed-tools: Bash(…)` rule matchable.

**Still not driven live:** the hook-decision merge, the hook-set session name,
skill-over-command precedence, and the plugin update badge. They need a session
whose project is the fixture directory, or a plugin with an update available.

## Run of 2026-08-07 (end of the doc-sweep parity wave)

Rebuilt BOTH bundles first — `electron:build:servers` and `electron:build:main`
— because R73 left the packaged Pi bundle stale and live evidence taken against
a stale subprocess is not evidence.

| case | result |
| :-- | :-- |
| `AGT-1` | **PASS** 10/10 — `/subtask` inherits the parent conversation, a named subagent given the same prompt cannot answer it, and no `/fork` entry exists |
| `AGT-2` | **PASS** 14/14 — fork panel: row per fork, transcript opens and is readable WHILE running, follow-up reaches the fork, stop and dismiss work |
| `AGT-3` | **PASS** 7/7 — a fork's permission prompt reaches the user, is attributed to the fork, and answering it unblocks the fork |
| `AGT-4` | **5/7, was BLOCKED** — unblocked by pointing it at the existing `custom:cdp-probe` style. The output-style half is now proven live: the style applied, **the fork inherited it and a named subagent did NOT** — the documented distinction. Rewind works (11 → 1). The two open checks are unimplemented halves OF THE CASE, not product failures: the memory half needs a marker already in the operator's own memory (the harness must not write there), and the rewind half's file assertion is not driven yet |
| `AGT-5`…`AGT-8` | **BLOCKED on fixtures**, each for a stated reason — a high-effort subagent, two installed hooks, a plugin with a `userConfig`, and a deployed managed policy. Each would pass vacuously without its fixture, which is why the harness refuses rather than scoring them |

Two incidental confirmations worth keeping:

- The output-style list read back as `["default","concise","explanatory","reviewer","proactive","learning","custom:cdp-probe"]` — **`proactive` and `learning` are R44's two added built-ins, live.**
- **`marketplace.listItems()` did not return within 180 s** on this host. That is
  exactly the behaviour R81 refused to put on the per-turn path (it awaits a
  delivery step, can kick a git clone onto the request path, and spends the
  registry retry budget), and it is why the update badge reads a cached-only
  `peekCachedItems()` instead. Live confirmation of a decision that was argued
  from the code.

No renderer errors and no uncaught exceptions in the whole session log.

**Not covered live, and honestly so:** `PLG-N9`/`PLG-N10` need an installed
plugin whose catalog advertises a newer version, and no such fixture exists on
this host — so R82's badge and Update action have unit and typecheck evidence
but no live pass. `HOK-N1`…`HOK-N4`, `SKL-N3`, `SKL-N4` and `CMD-N4` likewise
need probe plugins, skills and hook config written into the operator's own live
settings, which the harness deliberately does not do.

**The wave runs in `electron:dev`** — that is the decision, and it is the right
one for driving behaviour. What matters is knowing the one thing dev cannot
prove, so nobody mistakes a green wave for a green release:

> **Dev cannot prove the interceptor reaches the shipped app.**
> `resolveInterceptorBundlePath` preloads the `.ts` source when `!isPackaged`
> and `dist/interceptor.cjs` when packaged, returning `undefined` if the bundle
> is missing — and the Pi subprocess then starts **unpatched, with no error**,
> taking the `Nestor-Token` rewrite, the correlation headers and the SSE
> tool-call consolidation with it. In dev the interceptor is loaded from source
> **always**, so there is nothing there to break.

**Half of that gap is now closed by a build assertion, and the other half is
not.** `scripts/electron-build-main.ts:167` calls `assertInterceptorShips()` →
`verifyInterceptorBundle(INTERCEPTOR_OUTPUT, ELECTRON_INTERCEPTOR_CONTEXT)`
(`scripts/build/interceptor-pairing.ts:74`), so the Electron build now fails
when `dist/interceptor.cjs` is missing, empty or is not the interceptor. That is
the **bundle-ships** half. The **bundle-loads** half has no Electron analogue:
the headless server build additionally asserts that its entry preloads the
bundle (`interceptorPreloadFragment`, `interceptor-pairing.ts:111` — "The bundle
would ship but never load into the server process.") and that the Dockerfile
`ENTRYPOINT` routes through that entry (`:118-126`). Nothing checks that the
packaged Electron main still passes `interceptorBundlePath` to the Pi spawn
(`packages/shared/src/agent/backend/internal/runtime-resolver.ts:465`), so a
regression there reproduces exactly the silent failure described above and every
build stays green. **That residual stays filed.**

**Two facts about driving a PACKAGED app, if anyone ever needs to:**
`NESSY_CDP_PORT` is read only by `scripts/electron-dev.ts`, which turns it into
`--remote-debugging-port`; the app itself does not read it, so a packaged run
needs the flag passed to the binary directly. And `app.requestSingleInstanceLock()`
means a second copy **quits silently** while your normal instance is open — which
looks exactly like "CDP is not responding".

## How to run

```bash
# 1. build the bundles the dev app runs from — BOTH, and relaunch after either
bun run electron:build:servers   # the Pi subprocess
bun run electron:build:main      # the main process

# 2. start it
NESSY_CDP_PORT=9224 bun run electron:dev

# 3. drive it
NESSY_CDP_PORT=9224 bun run scripts/cdp/run.ts          # the wave
NESSY_CDP_PORT=9224 bun run scripts/cdp/cases/agt-1.ts  # one case
```

`scripts/cdp/README.md` documents the harness. Each case prints one machine line:
`##CDP-RESULT## {json}`, and exits 0 / 1 / 2 for PASS / FAIL / BLOCKED. **One id,
one row** — a `##CDP-RESULT##` keyed on an id that appears twice cannot be
attributed, which is why `PRM-F1` and `MCP-N10` were renumbered out of collisions
below. `AGT-1`…`AGT-10`, `MCP-W1`…`MCP-W3` and `SKL-P1` have driver scripts today
(the `CASES` array in `scripts/cdp/run.ts`, which `harness.test.ts` pins against
the files on disk); every other row is driven by hand until one is written. The
`MCP-W` cases and `SKL-P1` additionally need their own fixture tree and their own
`NESSY_CONFIG_DIR` (`fixtures/mcp-naming.ts` and `fixtures/paths-skill.ts`
respectively — two DIFFERENT roots and two different config dirs, so they are not
one wave), so a bare `bun run scripts/cdp/run.ts` against an ordinary config dir
reports them BLOCKED rather than failing — run them as named subsets.

`SKL-P1` automates only the METADATA half of its row; the activation half is
operator-run and the case says so in its own `activation-half` observation. A
green `SKL-P1` is not a `paths`-gating pass.

## The cases

Status: `⬜` not run in this cycle · `✅` passed · `⚠️` partial · `❗` failed · `🔒` blocked on its fixture

Priority: `🔴` blocks a release to testers · `🟡` should have · blank = standing regression

**A new case gets a row here first.** Feature, what it proves in one sentence,
what it needs to run. A case with no row is a case nobody will run.

### Claude runtime (`claude-runtime`) & the shared adapter — 8 blocking, run on a PACKAGED build

**This section is the one exception to "the wave runs in `electron:dev`", and the
exception is the point.** `claude-runtime` ships a ~247 MB native `claude` binary
that only a packaged artifact carries, behind a build-time flag esbuild bakes in
(`isClaudeRuntimeEnabled` reads `process.env.NESSY_FEATURE_CLAUDE_RUNTIME`
statically so `--define` can rewrite it) — a dev run proves neither.

Driving a packaged app, in full (the header's two facts, plus the two this pass
needed):

```bash
# the app does NOT read NESSY_CDP_PORT — pass the Chromium flag directly
NESSY_IS_PACKAGED=false \
NESSY_CONFIG_DIR=/private/tmp/t8/config \
"release/mac-arm64/Nessy Desktop.app/Contents/MacOS/Nessy Desktop" \
  --remote-debugging-port=9333
```

- `NESSY_CONFIG_DIR` gives the run a **virgin config dir**, so the one-click card
  is exercised from onboarding instead of against an existing connection — and
  the operator's real `~/.nessy-agent` is never touched. `~/.nessy/dp_auth_creds.json`
  stays shared with nessy-cli and READ-ONLY to this app; the card reuses it and
  no fixture may edit it.
- `NESSY_IS_PACKAGED=false` turns on `logger.ts` debug transports **without**
  making the app behave like a dev build: `logger.ts` is evaluated at import,
  and `main/index.ts:118` overwrites the variable with `'1'` before any
  subprocess spawns. That one line is what puts
  `Claude runtime adapter listening on 127.0.0.1:<port> (upstream: <kind>)` and
  `[ClaudeAdapter] result message: … stop_reason=…` in the log — the only
  in-app view of the adapter.
- `--debug` does NOT work: Electron hands it to Node, which rejects it
  (`[DEP0062] node --debug is invalid`) and the app never starts.
- Force dark first (`localStorage['nessy-theme'] = {"mode":"dark",…}` + reload);
  CDP emulates a light `prefers-color-scheme` and `mode: 'system'` flips the app
  white.

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|

To settle it, `lsof -p <pid>` DURING the call, not after. | — |

### Cycle 2026-08-06 verdicts — `CBX-1…7` + `CLR-10` re-run, packaged no-flag build (C4)

Run on `NessyDesktop-1.18.0-mac-arm64` built from `ee84fe4e` with NO feature
flags — after the default flip that build IS the shipping shape (both runtimes
bundled: `claude` 247 124 336 B, `codex-app-server` 207 037 840 B; onboarding
screenshot shows both runtime cards + the BYO card and no subscription cards).

- **7 ✅ / 0 ⚠️ / 0 ❗**, plus `CLR-10` upgraded ⚠️→✅ on its deterministic rig.
- **Four rig facts that cost this cycle real time, recorded so the next one
  doesn't pay again:**
  1. **`NESSY_CONFIG_DIR` must not sit under a symlinked ancestor.** Storage
     readiness (`assertSafeParent`, storage-readiness.ts) walks every ancestor
     and blocks on any symlink — `/tmp` IS one on macOS, so the T8 recipe's
     `/tmp/t8/config` now hangs startup on an invisible blocked-dialog. Use
     `/private/tmp/...`.
  2. **An occluded window starves the UI under CDP.** Chromium throttles rAF to
     zero when the window is fully covered (`document.visibilityState:
     "hidden"`), and `runAfterMenuClose` defers dialog-opening through
     `requestAnimationFrame` — so row-menu Edit silently never opens, and a
     stuck Radix `body{pointer-events:none}` lock never clears. Launch the app
     with `--disable-renderer-backgrounding --disable-backgrounding-occluded-windows`
     for a driven run, and treat `visibilityState` as a precondition.
  3. **Screenshots of an occluded window are STALE compositor frames** —
     `Page.captureScreenshot` happily returns the last painted frame, which can
     show a dialog the DOM closed a minute ago. DOM reads are the truth; a
     screenshot is only evidence when `visibilityState` is `visible`.
  4. **The single-instance lock makes a relaunch race look like a CDP failure**
     (the header already warns this): `pkill` + immediate relaunch had the new
     instance exit 0 silently while the old one unwound. Kill −9, wait for
     `pgrep` empty, then launch.
- The stuck pointer-events lock after settings dialogs is filed as its own
  follow-up (chip 2026-08-06); it reproduces on this build and blocks every
  subsequent click until a reload.

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `CMD-N1` | **R36 /btw:** typed `/btw what file did we just edit?` during a RUNNING turn answers in the dismissible overlay without interrupting the turn, `managed.messages` length is UNCHANGED after the exchange (read via `getSessions()`), and the session file on disk did not grow; bare `/btw` afterwards REOPENS the overlay on that exchange; `Left`/`Right` step across two accumulated answers; `c` puts raw Markdown on the clipboard; `x` clears the earlier list (interactive-mode.md:359-385). | a session with ≥2 turns of real history; a long-running turn for the mid-turn half |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `CMD-N2` | **R36 /recap:** `/recap` returns a ONE-LINE summary in the overlay, transcript unchanged; on the `nessy-cloud-agent` connection both `/btw` and `/recap` are ABSENT from the slash menu (excludedCommandIds) (interactive-mode.md:398-404). | same session; a Nestor Cloud connection for the negative |
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `CMD-N3` | **R36 /clear:** `/clear` on a session with history empties the visible transcript (the renderer refetches on completion) and the NEXT turn's model context starts empty — ask for the pre-clear codeword and the model must not know it. On codex the next turn does NOT resume the old thread; on claude-runtime the stored SDK session id is dropped. **On disk the stored `session.jsonl` is REWRITTEN to exactly one line, its header, with zero messages** (the complete handler empties `managed.messages`, `enqueuePersist` snapshots the emptied array and `persistence-queue` writes header+messages atomically). Assert the shrink to one line; do NOT assert the old conversation survives — that is what the R36 plan intended and what the write path does not do, and the discrepancy is worth filing separately. Also check what the user is left with: the `Context cleared — the next prompt starts a fresh conversation.` info event is yielded BEFORE the messages are emptied, so confirm it is still visible after the refetch. | a session with a memorable codeword planted pre-clear; run once per backend (pi / claude-runtime / codex-runtime); `wc -l` the session.jsonl before and after |
| ⬜ | | `CMD-N4` | **The `/btw` error surface, which the success path never touches.** Type `/btw does this work?` on a freshly created session where no turn has run: the overlay opens and renders a READABLE inline error (`agent-not-started`), not an empty or endlessly spinning card, and the composer returns to normal afterwards. The typed-error DTO carries `session-not-found`, `empty-question`, `agent-not-started` and provider errors — the whole reason its shape exists — and `CMD-N1` covers only success. | a brand-new session, opened and left un-prompted |

Any suggestion that does render sits BELOW the breakdown and names a control that exists. | — |

The section otherwise covers only AUTO compaction, so the manual command's custom-instruction path is undriven. | a session whose history contains two clearly separable topics, only one of them auth |
| ⬜ |  | `CMP-N4` | **The thrashing terminal reason actually fires (positive control for CMP-N1).** Force compaction to succeed and have a large tool output immediately refill the window three times running: the turn must END with the thrashing message rather than loop forever. `CMP-N1` asserts this reason must NOT appear — an absence with nothing on the same rig proving it can. | the `CMP-N1` env plus a tool call returning a very large payload on every turn |
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `CMP-N6` | **A session command goes out BARE, measured on the artifact users download.** The 2026-08-11 proof of this read `[session] Message: /compact` out of the dev app's stdout, and that instrument does not exist on a packaged build — `apps/electron/src/main/logger.ts` sets both electron-log transports to `false` whenever `isDebugMode` is false, which on a packaged Electron build it always is; the independent audit of the signed 1.25.0 looked and found only `<config>/logs/auto-update.log`. Two further reasons not to rebuild that detector: the line prints the RAW incoming `message`, never the `effectiveMessage` handed to `agent.chat()`, so on its own it never proved "bare"; and it needs `--debug`, which changes the build it measures. **The instrument that does exist is the runtime's own session file, and the helper is `scripts/cdp/lib/runtime-session-log.ts`.** Read it before dispatching `/compact` and again once the session is idle (`bareCommandEvidence`), then assert all three together: a `compaction` entry APPEARED (the effect the product promises); ZERO new user `message` entries; and no entry carrying `<available_skills>`, `<session_state>`, `<system-reminder>` or `<capability-generation>`. A session command never becomes a model turn, so a decorated one would have been sent as ordinary prose and would show up as exactly the user entry this asserts is absent — positive and negative in one read, from a file the feature writes itself. `compactions > 0` ALONE is satisfied by an auto-compaction landing in the same window; all three checks or none. The transcript's own `info` row — `Compacted context to fit within limits (from ~N tokens)`, the string `SessionManager` keys `compaction_complete` off — corroborates from the UI side and is worth recording, but it is the app's account of itself, so it never substitutes for the sidecar read. **A `/compact` that reached the model as prose would instead answer with an assistant turn ABOUT the word "/compact"**; that is the shape to look for when a run goes red. **Runtime coverage is not uniform and must be reported per arm:** Pi writes `<config>/sessions/<workspaceId>/<sessionId>/.pi-sessions/*.jsonl`; the Claude runtime writes `<config>/workspaces/<workspaceId>/claude-config/projects/…/*.jsonl`; **Codex writes nothing readable** — `codex-agent.ts` mints its `CODEX_HOME` with `mkdtempSync` and `rmSync`es it on teardown, so the Codex arm has NO instrument on a packaged build and stays a named gap. That matters because the 2026-08-11 measurement was taken on a Codex session. | any session with enough history that `/compact` does not answer "session too small" (the trap `AGT-6` hit twice); one arm per runtime, and the Codex arm recorded as instrument-less rather than skipped |
| ✅ | 🔴 | `COMPACT-LIFE` | **The whole compaction lifecycle, per runtime (F5).** Fill a session to roughly HALF the effective window, invoke MANUAL compaction there and watch the context come back down; then keep filling until AUTO-compaction fires on its own and watch it drop again. One arm per run (`NESSY_F5_RUNTIME=pi\|claude\|codex`) because each needs a different connection and its own detector. The window is narrowed through the PRODUCT path — a small `contextWindow` on a RUN-OWNED connection's `models[]` entry, which live-proves C2's sync consumption, F2's `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` and F3's `model_context_window` end to end — never `NESSY_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW` (it narrows Pi's compaction CAPACITY while leaving `contextUsage.contextWindow` at the model's real 262 144, so "half the window" and "the threshold" would be computed against two different numbers) and never the claude env var (F2 spreads `envOverrides` after `process.env`, so a launch-time value is silently clobbered). Detectors: Pi = `compaction` entries in `.pi-sessions/*.jsonl`, whose own `tokensBefore` is what the threshold assertion compares against; claude = `compact_boundary` in the SDK transcript, narrowed to the project directory whose name ends with this session's id; codex = the occupancy sequence alone, recorded as instrument-less rather than skipped. **Size the window from the tail, not from the base:** compaction keeps `keepRecentTokens` = 20 000 tokens of CONVERSATION, and the ~70 600-token resting base is not conversation, so half the window must clear the base by more than that tail or the runtime correctly answers "Nothing to compact (session too small)". **Size the last APPROACH turn too, not just the window** — a threshold is the bottom of a band whose top is where the request stops fitting, and on claude that band (207 000…230 144) is narrower than one 60 000-character filler, so a fixed step jumps it and the run measures a refused request instead of a compaction. **Result: all three runtimes PASS — pi 10/10, codex 9/9 after F7, claude 13/13 after F9.** The claude arm needed three sittings: its second measured no automatic compaction at 94.7% of its own window (with the manual command in the same session as the positive control), F9 then found the cause in the bundled SDK binary (an unrecognised model id resolves to auto-compact window source `"auto"`, which the SDK's own gate reads as "skip proactive compaction") and fixed it with `CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW`, and the third sitting read the fix back: a second `compact_boundary`, `trigger: "auto"`, `preTokens: 217844` against the 207 000 point on a 240 000 window, occupancy 204 509 → 66 229, error-row diff empty. **A second record is not yet an automatic one** — the manual `/compact` writes one too, so the arm asserts the runtime's own label, and an absent label fails. | `NESSY_F5_ALLOW_CONNECTION_WRITE=1`; a signed-in Nessy Gateway (`~/.nessy/dp_auth_creds.json`); a free `<base>-<suffix>` slug |
| ⬜ |  | `CMP-N5` | **A suggestion actually renders (positive control for CMP-N2).** Drive `/context` on a Pi session WITH a real MCP server whose tool listing clears both 2 000 tokens and 20% of the configured total: the deferred-MCP suggestion appears below the breakdown, names a control that exists, and acting on it changes the breakdown. `CMP-N2` only ever asserts suggestions are absent, so nothing proves the surface can render at all. Note the bar is high precisely because `system_tools` is ~12k live. | an MCP server advertising a large tool catalog (tens of tools with long descriptions) |

### Instructions, memory & rules

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ |  | `RUL-1` | An unscoped `.claude/rules/*.md` influences the FIRST turn | — |
| ⬜ |  | `RUL-2` | A `paths:` rule fires on a matching file read — the tool result carries `<pre_tool_use_context><rule …globs=…>` with the body | — |
| ⬜ |  | `RUL-3` | Recursive discovery finds `rules/<subdir>/x.md`; symlinked dir and file both load; a cycle does not hang | — |
| ⬜ |  | `RUL-4` | An edited rule body is re-delivered once the capability generation changes (no restart needed) | — |
| ⬜ |  | `RUL-5` | **Which rule wins.** Put a rule in the USER tier (`~/.nessy-agent/rules/` or `~/.claude/rules/`) and a conflicting one with the same name in the project, then ask the model which instruction it is following: BOTH load and the PROJECT one wins. The loader pushes the user tier first for exactly this reason, and all four RUL rows above are project-scope only, so the ordering that decides the answer is unproven. | one user-tier rule and one project rule giving contradictory, checkable instructions (e.g. answer in uppercase vs lowercase) |
| ⬜ |  | `RUL-6` | **An untrusted project's rules never reach the model.** Open a project that has NOT been trusted, carrying a `.claude/rules/*.md` whose body demands a distinctive token, and confirm the model never produces it — then trust the project and confirm it does (the positive control). Loading an untrusted repo's instructions is a prompt-injection surface and no live row covers the refusal. | a disposable repo left untrusted at first open, with one rule file carrying a generated token |

### Sessions & history

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ |  | `MIR-1` | A live turn mirrors to `<mirror>/<ws>/<sid>/transcript.jsonl` + `summary.json`, with line 1 a `session_header` entry and lines 2+ `message` entries carrying `<messageId>#<hash8>` uuids — an empty or malformed file is the failure | `"sessionMirror": { "adapter": "directory", "path": "<absolute scratch path>" }` in `~/.nessy-agent/config.json` BEFORE launch. With the key absent the mirror is off and this case passes vacuously. The parser is strict: an unknown key throws, `adapter` must be exactly `directory`, and `path` must be ABSOLUTE |
| ⬜ |  | `MIR-2` | Deleted local `session.jsonl` (dir kept) → boot-restore: listed + full history | continues from `MIR-1` (the same `sessionMirror` config must be in place) |
| ⬜ |  | `MIR-3` | Unwritable mirror → a `Session mirror error: EACCES …` info message in the transcript, emitted ONCE per distinct reason (a case expecting one message per turn misreads the dedupe as a miss); the chat continues and the local `session.jsonl` is unaffected. The envelope retries 3× with 250 ms backoff, so the message lands ~0.5 s after the turn, not instantly | `MIR-1`'s config pointing at a root made unwritable (`chmod 500`) before the turn — use a FRESH session or a fresh mirror root if you want the `mkdir` form of the message rather than the `open` form |
| ⬜ |  | `MIR-4` | Mirror across a real restart with a warm session: the restart makes the in-process uuid cache cold and every entry is re-offered, and the transcript must NOT double (entries are content-addressed and deduped by uuid) | continues from `MIR-1` |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `MIR-N1` | Launch the app against a config dir holding sessions last used past the cutoff: those session dirs are GONE, a session inside the window survives, and `.pi-processes`, `history.jsonl` and `config.json` are untouched. Settings → App → Data retention then reports the effective window (plus a managed note when policy sets it), and shows the pause warning instead when the config is unreadable. The card and `nessy-general-agent doctor` report the DECISION and never a removal count — the sweep-status record with the counts has no production reader, and a doctor process never runs a sweep. | a scratch `NESSY_CONFIG_DIR` seeded with copies of real session dirs whose header `lastUsedAt` is back-dated past the cutoff (back-dating `archivedAt` does nothing unless `isArchived: true` is also set), plus one session inside the window, a `.pi-processes` marker and a `history.jsonl`. **The scratch `config.json` must list the seeded workspace in `workspaceRefs`** — the sweep is restricted to registered workspaces, so an unregistered one is never swept and the case passes with nothing deleted. Cold launch required: the sweep runs once per process |
| ⬜ |  | `MIR-5` | **Cleared content stays cleared through a mirror rebuild.** Mirror a session, `/clear` it, delete the local `session.jsonl`, relaunch, and confirm the restored transcript does NOT contain the pre-clear codeword. The mirror writes a `session_reset` marker whenever a snapshot drops previously-mirrored message ids, precisely so a rebuild never resurrects deleted content; `MIR-2` proves restore works but nothing proves this. | `MIR-1`'s config, plus a codeword planted before the `/clear` |
| ⬜ |  | `MIR-6` | **A malformed mirror config fails safe at PARSE time.** Write a `sessionMirror` block with an unknown adapter into `~/.nessy-agent/config.json` and launch: the app STARTS, chat persists locally as normal, and the `[SessionMirror] Invalid sessionMirror config — mirroring disabled (local persistence unaffected)` diagnostic appears. `MIR-3` covers a mirror that fails at WRITE time; this is the likelier user error and it sits on the startup path. | `"sessionMirror": { "adapter": "s3", "path": "/tmp/x" }` in the user config |

### MessageDisplay hook — 1 blocking

`whats-new/2026-w22.md:108`: the event "lets hooks transform or hide assistant
message text as it is displayed". `hooks.md:876` makes it display-only — "the
transcript and what Claude sees keep the original".

The invariant that matters is the one a unit test cannot reach: the replacement must
never become the transcript. Verify by reading the session file on disk, not just the
screen.

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `MD-1` | A `MessageDisplay` hook replacing `sk-` strings → the chat shows the redaction, and the session JSONL on disk contains the ORIGINAL. Ask the model about its own previous message: it must see the original | a hook doing `gsub("sk-[A-Za-z0-9]+"; "[redacted]")` |
| ⬜ |  | `MD-2` | Copy, pop-out and "format as markdown" on the redacted turn all yield the ORIGINAL (`hooks.md:1273`, "verbose mode shows the original") | continues from `MD-1` |
| ⬜ |  | `MD-3` | Every failure path renders the original: hook absent, exit non-zero with no output, hook that throws, hook that exceeds the 10 s timeout (`hooks.md:740`) | a hook with `sleep 15` |
| ⬜ |  | `MD-4` | A Claude plugin bundling a `MessageDisplay` hook installs clean — the Plugins page no longer reports `plugin.deferred.event_message_display_unsupported` | such a bundle |
| ⬜ |  | `MD-5` | With a slow hook active, a long streamed answer arrives in order — no line appears before an earlier one | a hook with `sleep 0.3` |
| ⬜ |  | `MD-6` | **Reload shows the original — the cheapest proof the redaction never became the transcript.** With the `MD-1` hook still active, reload or reopen the session: the chat now shows the ORIGINAL `sk-` text, because the display replacement is renderer-only and never survives a reload. Cheaper and more legible than `MD-1`'s read-the-JSONL step, and asserted by no row today. | continues from `MD-1` |
| ⬜ |  | `MD-7` | **The NATIVE MessageDisplay path.** Create a `MessageDisplay` automation through the app's own Automations UI, with no plugin involved, and confirm it redacts on screen exactly as the plugin-bundled hook does. All five MD rows above drive the imported-plugin path only, so the native path could break with nothing failing. | an automation created in-app with the same `sk-` replacement |

### Auto-memory load caps — 1 blocking

`memory.md:384`: "The first 200 lines of `MEMORY.md`, or the first 25KB,
whichever comes first, load at session start." Before R33 **neither** cap shipped.
The line cap existed but only on `loadMemoryPromptBlock`, a composer both backends
skip whenever a capability runtime is set — which is every normal turn — and the
byte cap had no representation in the repo at all. So the index loaded whole, on
both live deliveries.

Measured on a real curated index grown 12× by ordinary `remember` use: 64 883 B →
25 493 B, ~15 990 → ~6 284 estimated tokens. On realistic prose entries the BYTE
cap binds first, 25 lines before the line cap — the half that was missing is the
half that governs.

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `ML-1` | With a `MEMORY.md` over 25 KB, start a session on the **Nessy · Pi agent** card (the `memory_files` readout is produced only by the Pi subprocess) and run `/context` → in the **Loaded files** group, the per-file `MEMORY.md` row reads the CAPPED body, ~6 300 tokens, not the ~16 000 of the whole file. Read the PER-FILE row, not the **Memory files** category: that category legitimately exceeds the cap because it also sums the instruction layers (CLAUDE.md, AGENTS.md, `.claude/rules`), and pinning the expectation on it produces a false FAIL in any real project. Run on a PACKAGED build: `electron:dev` runs the Pi subprocess from a built bundle and can preload unpatched with no error | a `MEMORY.md` of ~64 KB, ~440 lines |
| ⬜ |  | `ML-2` | The cut lands on a whole line — the last surviving entry is not half an entry. Observe it by asking the model to quote the LAST entry it can see in its loaded index; the `/context` overlay shows a token count, never the body | continues from `ML-1` |
| ⬜ |  | `ML-3` | A `CLAUDE.md` and a `.claude/rules/*.md` of the same size load in FULL. `memory.md:390` exempts them, and the caps must not leak onto the rule reader | a 40 KB `CLAUDE.md` in a project that has been TRUSTED — the `.claude/rules` leg is read only for a trusted project, so an untrusted repo passes that half vacuously |
| ⬜ |  | `ML-4` | **An `@import` past the byte cut is not followed.** With a `MEMORY.md` over 25 KB whose `@topic.md` import line sits AFTER the cut, ask the model to name that topic file's content: it must not know it, while an import line placed BEFORE the cut is followed normally (the positive control). The cap is applied before `@path` expansion and the limits are deliberately not threaded into the recursion — a real, user-visible consequence of the caps that `ML-1`…`ML-3` never touch. | the `ML-1` memory file with two `@topic.md` imports, one near the top and one at the end, each pointing at a file carrying a distinct token |
| ⬜ |  | `ML-5` | **The cap on the fork's other default coding runtime.** With the same over-cap `MEMORY.md`, start a **Claude Code (Nessy Gateway)** session and ask the model to list every heading in its loaded memory index: headings past the cut must be ABSENT, and headings before it present. The cap itself is backend-agnostic, but the `/context` readout `ML-1` relies on exists only on Pi, so this is the only way to see it here. | the `ML-1` memory file, with numbered headings so "past the cut" is unambiguous |

### Worktree lifecycle — 1 blocking

Three contracts landed as R38. `worktrees.md:150-162`: a `.worktreeinclude`
file at the project root names gitignored files to copy into every
git-created worktree — before R38 the file had NO reader and failed silently.
`worktrees.md:128`: a `"fresh"` base keeps `origin/HEAD` current (24h
staleness → fetch, 5s cap, cached-ref fallback) — before R38 the ref was only
ever read, so an unfetched checkout branched worktrees off a stale base with
no warning. `large-codebases.md:245`: `extensions.worktreeConfig` is removed
after the last worktree goes, only when we enabled it — before R38 the entry
stayed forever and go-git tools (`tea`) refuse to open such a repository.

**Every row here needs a Pi-backed session.** `EnterWorktree`, `ExitWorktree` and
`Task` are `visibility: 'pi-only'` (a security constraint, not a preference) and
the lease service is `piWorktreeLeaseServices`, so on the Claude Code or Codex
cards no worktree is ever created and every row fails for the wrong reason.

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `WTR-1` | In a repo with a gitignored `.env` (mode 0600) and a `.worktreeinclude` listing it, `EnterWorktree` (and a subagent with `isolation: worktree`) yields a worktree where `.env` EXISTS, carries the same content, and keeps mode 0600. A TRACKED file matching the same pattern must carry its COMMITTED content in the worktree, not the uncommitted edit sitting in the main checkout — that dirty content is the copy that must not happen, and without the local edit the arm cannot fail at all because `git worktree add` checks tracked files out regardless. Without `.worktreeinclude` the worktree has no `.env` (the negative control). PACKAGED build — `electron:dev` can preload stale bundles. | a scratch repo: `.gitignore` with `.env`, `.env` chmod 600, `.worktreeinclude` listing `.env`, and one TRACKED file matching the pattern with an uncommitted edit in the main checkout |
| ⬜ |  | `WTR-2` | With `worktree.baseRef` unset (fresh default) in a repo whose `FETCH_HEAD` is >24h old and whose remote has moved: creating a worktree runs a `fetch` first (≤5s) and the worktree branches from the REMOTE default tip, not the stale cached one; with the remote unreachable the same creation still succeeds off the cached ref. | a clone with an artificially old `FETCH_HEAD` (touch -t) and a remote advanced by one commit; a Pi session |
| ⬜ |  | `WTR-3` | With `worktree.sparsePaths` set: after the LAST worktree is removed, `git config --local --get extensions.worktreeConfig` in the main checkout is EMPTY; when the user had set the value themselves BEFORE the first worktree, it survives removal (the never-remove-yours control). | a repo with `worktree.sparsePaths: ["src"]` in `~/.nessy-agent/config.json`; second run with `git config extensions.worktreeConfig true` pre-set; a Pi session |
| ⬜ |  | `WTR-4` | **Coming back out.** On a Pi session already inside a worktree, ask the agent to leave it: the session's working directory returns to the path the session started in and the chat's cwd indicator shows the original repo root again. `ExitWorktree` is the other half of the affordance `WTR-1` exercises, and a worktree the user cannot leave is the failure they would actually hit. | continues from `WTR-1` |
| ⬜ |  | `WTR-5` | **`bgIsolation` blocks the main checkout.** With `worktree.bgIsolation: "worktree"` in `~/.nessy-agent/config.json`, run a background/subagent turn that tries to Edit or Write a file in the MAIN checkout: it must be REFUSED, and the same edit must succeed after `EnterWorktree`. It is the one worktree setting with a direct block-the-user consequence and it has no row. | the `WTR-1` repo plus the config key; a Pi session |
| ⬜ |  | `WTR-6` | **Symlinked directories.** With `worktree.symlinkDirectories: ["node_modules"]`, create a worktree and confirm `node_modules` inside it is a SYMLINK to the main checkout's copy. Then delete the target and create another worktree: creation must still SUCCEED (a missing target is skipped, not fatal) — that is the control. Third of the three `worktree.*` keys and the only one with no row. | the `WTR-1` repo with a real `node_modules`; a Pi session |
| ⬜ |  | `WTR-7` | **The documented exception to `WTR-1`.** With a `WorktreeCreate` hook that returns a DIFFERENT worktree path, create a worktree in a repo that has a `.worktreeinclude`: the gitignored file must NOT be copied. Without a row, `WTR-1` can pass while this exception silently inverts, which puts secrets in a directory nobody expected. | the `WTR-1` repo plus a `WorktreeCreate` hook printing an alternative path; a Pi session |

### Managed settings key vocabulary — 1 blocking

R31 declared documented Claude Code settings keys our strict parser previously
rejected. The severity was not the missing feature: `StoredConfigTopLevelSchema`
is `.strict()` and a failed parse THROWS, so one documented-but-undeclared key
cost the user **every** setting in `config.json`. These cases prove the file
loads and that each key now has a consequence.

Managed cases need a managed policy file on the host-owned path — see
`docs/architecture/managed-mcp-access.md` for where it lives per platform.

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `MS-1` | Put `claudeMdExcludes` and `env` in `config.json`, restart → the app starts with **all** other settings intact. Before R31 this threw and every setting was lost | a config with both keys plus two unrelated settings |
| ⬜ |  | `MS-2` | `claudeMdExcludes` at user AND `.claude/settings.local.json` scope → both patterns are excluded (union, per `settings.md:696` "concatenated and deduplicated, not replaced"), not just the higher-priority one | a TRUSTED project with a `CLAUDE.md` and a `.claude/rules/*.md` — the local-scope leg contributes only for a trusted project, so an untrusted repo makes the union look broken |
| ⬜ |  | `MS-3` | A user-scope `env` block in `config.json` does NOT reach the agent's subprocess environment (a turn that echoes the named variable finds it unset), and `nessy-general-agent doctor` lists it under **Rejected settings layers** naming the variable but never its value. **This case pins a deliberate park** (`docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-04-user-env-park.md`) — if it ever passes by the variable appearing, that is the park being silently "finished". The doctor half is a CLI check: there is no renderer surface for rejected layers | `env` naming a variable that is ABSENT from the ambient environment (otherwise the echo resolves for an unrelated reason), then a turn that echoes it |
| ⬜ |  | `MS-4` | Managed `strictPluginOnlyCustomization: ["skills","agents"]` → user- and project-scope skills and agents disappear from the lists while MCP servers and hooks stay. `true` locks all four. Bundled skills are exempt by design and remain visible under a locked `skills` surface — do not read them as a failure | one user skill, one user agent, one user MCP server |
| ⬜ |  | `MS-5` | On a **Pi session with the guardian enabled and auto mode active**, driven by a GENERAL request and never a direct ask for the action (explicit user intent overrides soft-deny by design — "clean up the repo" does not authorize a force-push, "force-push this branch" does, and a direct prompt fails the case for the wrong reason): managed `autoMode.soft_deny: ["My rule", "$defaults"]` → the fixture shell action is BLOCKED with the guardian's diagnostic (category + reason), and an action matching only `My rule` is blocked the same way. It is BLOCKED, not held for approval — a hold is the fallback state the guardian lands in when it is disabled, circuit-open or unsure, i.e. exactly the state in which this case proves nothing. Drop `$defaults` and the SAME built-in action now runs — the built-ins were REPLACED, the documented Danger case. A guardian-cleared allow is distinguishable from a plain rule-allow by its `guardian-approved<category>: <reason>` receipt provenance. The judge's prompt has no user-visible surface; assert the DECISION, not the text | a general request whose obvious execution trips a built-in soft block, plus one that trips only `My rule`. The verdict is model-produced: pick an action the built-in list names unambiguously and re-run on a single disagreement |
| ⬜ |  | `MS-6` | **`classifyAllShell` suspends allow rules.** With managed `autoMode.classifyAllShell: true` and a `permissions.allow` rule that would normally let a Bash command straight through, run that command in auto mode: it must still be routed to the classifier (held or judged), and with the key ABSENT the same command runs straight through (the control). It is a managed key in this section's vocabulary, it changes the security direction, and it has no row. | managed policy with the key; a workspace `allow Bash(...)` rule matching the fixture command; a Pi session in auto mode |
| ⬜ |  | `MS-7` | **The NEXT undeclared key.** Put a genuinely undeclared key in `~/.nessy-agent/config.json`, restart, and confirm the app names the offending file and key rather than silently starting on defaults with every setting lost. `MS-1` proves two specific keys were added to the vocabulary; nothing proves the behaviour when a new one arrives, which is the whole severity this section's header describes. | a config carrying two real settings plus one invented top-level key |

### Deep links — 2 blocking

A link Nessy did not produce must be inert. `deep-links.md`: "A deep link never
executes anything on its own. The link only chooses a directory and fills the
prompt box." Before R43 an OS- or web-delivered `nessy://action/...` reached the
renderer's full action switch, so `send=true` sent a prompt with no user step and
sibling actions deleted sessions and sources unprompted.

**Deliver the link the way an attacker would, not with an in-app control** — the
whole finding is about origin, so a case that navigates from inside the app proves
the opposite of what it claims. Use `open 'nessy://...'` from a terminal (macOS),
or a plain `<a href>` on a page loaded in Nessy's own browser pane.

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `DL-1` | `open 'nessy://action/new-session?input=INJECTED&send=true'` → a session opens with `INJECTED` **prefilled and unsent**, no turn starts, and a line under the input box reads `Prompt from an external link` | — |
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `DL-2` | `open 'nessy://action/delete-session/<real id>'` → the session still exists; the invalid-link toast appears. Repeat for `delete-source/<slug>` and `set-mode/<sid>?mode=bypassPermissions` (mode unchanged) | one throwaway session + one installed source |

Use a DIFFERENT sample for each attempt: a second click on the same sample is deliberately ignored by the launch-token dedupe, and reads as "nothing happened" | the browser pane open on Nessy's own browser empty-state page (the launch path is gated on that URL); at least two sample prompts available |

The compound-route branch never passes through the deep-link policy, so half the deep-link vocabulary is untested against this section's own "must be inert" claim. | a second workspace with one session in it, and a snapshot of both workspaces' session lists to compare after |

### Permissions & approvals — 1 blocking

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ⬜ |  | `APR-1` | Pending consent survives SIGKILL → re-fires on open through the production band | — |
| ⬜ |  | `APR-2` | Approve the re-fired card and the turn resumes: a NEW visible user message `Continue "<command>" after approval` appears (a tester who does not expect it reads it as a failure), the tool re-runs and its result lands in the transcript. The button reads **Allow** on an ordinary card, **Allow once** on an `outside_root` one, and **Once** only on a workflow-launch consent — the original row's "Once" is runnable against that card alone. Two more assertions no other row makes: on a hashed rememberable Bash prompt the re-issued call must NOT raise a second card (a second card there is the bug), and an ALLOW on an EXPIRED durable record must settle as a DENY with nothing executed | continues from `APR-1`; run once with a Bash prompt and once with a workflow-launch consent; for the expiry arm let the stored record age past its window before relaunching |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `APR-3` | Deny on a re-fired card ends the turn cleanly: nothing executes, no spinner is left behind, exactly one terminal `complete` (the original waiter died with the old process, so the manager emits the terminal event itself — without it the renderer sits on 'Responding…' forever). **Same run as `CNV-N3` in Conversation & turns: execute once and record the verdict in both rows** | continues from `APR-1` |
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `PRM-F1` | In **auto** mode with the guardian judge enabled AND provably answering, five floors still reach a human: `rm -rf ~` (removal circuit breaker), a write under `.claude/**` (protected path), an MCP tool flagged `_meta["anthropic/requiresUserInteraction"]`, an explicit `ask` rule, and an `EnterWorktree` outside the repo's worktree namespace. Plus the breaker's three properties: `rm -rf ~/nessy-cb-probe-does-not-exist` must NOT prompt (**the control** — without it a breaker that fires on everything reads as a pass); decline and re-ask the same command and it prompts AGAIN (no don't-ask-again — the breaker prompt deliberately carries neither `rememberForMinutes` nor `commandHash`); `sudo rm -rf ~` and `echo "$(rm -rf ~)"` each prompt; and a workspace `allow Bash(rm:*)` rule does not clear it. **Decline every one** — the prompt appearing is the proof, not the command running. Renamed from `PRM-N1`: that id belongs to R41's TaskStop row in *Permission classification*, and the `CB-1..CB-4` reference the old row carried died with `67918f1fa` (the prose is recoverable from `git show 7605ab2f4`) | auto mode, guardian ENABLED with a reachable judge model — show a guardian consult firing on one non-floor borderline action in the same session, because with the guardian off all five floors prompt trivially and the row passes proving nothing; a workspace `ask` rule and an `allow Bash(rm:*)` rule; one MCP server flagging a tool `requiresUserInteraction`; HOME pointed at a disposable directory (as `PRV-N5`) so an ABSENT breaker cannot destroy real data |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `PRM-N2a` | **Precedence.** In one session, drive one command through all five decision authorities in order: a `deny Bash(rm:*)` rule beats an `ask` rule, which beats the mode gate, which beats the guardian judge, which beats a plugin PreToolUse hook printing `{"permissionDecision":"allow"}` — and the transcript names which authority stopped it at each step | a workspace permissions file with one `deny Bash(rm:*)` and one `ask` rule, plus an installed plugin whose PreToolUse hook prints `permissionDecision: allow` |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `PRM-N2b` | **A hook `allow` cannot clear a floor.** With the same hook installed and allowing, a prompt still reaches the human for `rm -rf ~` (removal circuit breaker), a write under `.claude/**` (protected path) and an explicit `ask` rule. Decline each | same fixture as `PRM-N2a` |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `PRM-F2` | **The breaker survives the mode where nothing else prompts.** In `bypassPermissions` mode ask for `rm -rf ~/nessy-cb-probe-does-not-exist` — NO prompt (the control). Then ask for `rm -rf ~` — a prompt must appear even here, naming what would be removed. Decline, ask again, and it must prompt AGAIN. Add a workspace `allow Bash(rm:*)` rule and ask for `rm -rf /` — still a prompt. Try `sudo rm -rf ~` and `echo "$(rm -rf ~)"` — a prompt for each. Decline every one. This is the one class `allow-all` does not skip, and it is what distinguishes the breaker from the protected-path floor in `PRM-F3` | `bypassPermissions` mode; HOME pointed at a disposable directory so an absent breaker cannot destroy real data |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `PRM-F3` | **The protected-path floor, and where it stops.** With an `Edit(.claude/**)` ALLOW rule in the workspace permissions file, ask the model to edit a file under `.claude/` in `default` mode — a prompt must STILL appear, because the safety check runs before allow rules are evaluated. Repeat in `bypassPermissions` — this floor IS skipped there, unlike the removal breaker in `PRM-F2`. The bypass half is the control that tells the two floors apart; without it a passing row cannot distinguish them | a scratch repo with `.claude/` content and the allow rule in its workspace permissions file |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `PRM-F4` | **"Always Allow" does two different things and the tip must say which.** In a git repo, let a Bash command raise the card, READ THE TIP FIRST (it must say the approval is remembered per REPOSITORY), click **Always Allow**, then confirm `.claude/settings.local.json` at the repo root gained the rule and that the same command in a FRESH session in that repo runs with no prompt. Repeat with a file-modification prompt: the tip must say SESSION, and no file is written. A wrong tip makes a user grant a permanent repo-wide rule believing it lasts one session | a git repo used as the workspace, with `.claude/settings.local.json` absent beforehand so the write is unambiguous |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `PRM-F5` | **No button for a grant the host will not honour.** Raise a permission prompt for an approval that cannot be remembered (a Monitor WebSocket source) and confirm the card shows only **Allow** and **Deny** — no Always Allow button at all — and that the tip reads the "never" variant. A regression here offers a grant the host then ignores | a Monitor WebSocket source the model is asked to watch |
| ⬜ | 🟡 | `PRM-F6` | **The out-of-sandbox warning on an ordinary card.** With an administrator `excludedCommands` pattern configured, run a matching Bash command and confirm the ORDINARY permission card — not only the admin/sudo one — shows the warning-toned impact block saying the command runs outside the sandbox, positioned ABOVE the command preview. The placement is deliberate so a long command cannot push the warning out of view at the moment of the click, and neither the render nor the position is proven anywhere | a managed policy carrying an `excludedCommands` pattern, plus a command long enough that the preview scrolls |

### Agents & forks — 2 blocking

| | | case | what it proves | fixture |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| ✅ | 🔴 | `AGT-3` | A fork's permission prompt reaches the user AND answering it unblocks the fork | — |
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `SAD-1` | **R35 depth default:** in a fresh session, a subagent CAN dispatch a nested subagent, a third layer can too, and the fourth-layer attempt fails with `Subagent depth limit exceeded (3)` — the pre-R35 build refused the FIRST nested spawn (`sub-agents.md:867`, default three layers below main). Prove it BEHAVIOURALLY, by each layer actually delegating and reporting its child's answer: no field of the runtime snapshot exposes a child's tool list, so "its tool list carries `subagent`" is not directly readable. No driver script exists (`scripts/cdp/run.ts` knows only AGT-1..AGT-8), so this is a hand-driven row today. | a prompt chain asking each layer to delegate one question down and report its child's answer |
| ⬜ |  | `SAD-2` | **R35 env override:** relaunch with `NESSY_MAX_SUBAGENT_SPAWN_DEPTH=1` → a subagent cannot spawn at all (nesting off, the documented opt-out); with `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_SUBAGENT_SPAWN_DEPTH=2` alone the compat alias is honoured; with `NESSY_...=abc` the default 3 applies (malformed ignored, never unlimited) (`env-vars.md:281`). Hand-driven, as `SAD-1`. | same chain, three app relaunches with the env set on the ELECTRON MAIN process (the value is read at manifest seed time) |
| ⚠️ |  | `AGT-4a` | **Output style:** with a custom style active, a `/subtask` fork's recorded result carries the style's marker; a NAMED subagent's OWN transcript — read via `read_child`, never the parent's relay, because the parent is styled too and its relay carries the marker regardless — does not | `~/.nessy-agent/output-styles/cdp-probe.md` + `NESSY_CDP_OUTPUT_STYLE_ID=custom:cdp-probe NESSY_CDP_OUTPUT_STYLE_MARKER=…` |
| ⬜ |  | `AGT-4b` | **Rewind:** `rewindToMessage` with `restoreFiles:true` on the parent shortens the parent transcript AND reverts the file a fork wrote, while the file a named subagent wrote in the same session survives. The current driver hardcodes this assertion as a failure, so it can never report PASS until the fixture below exists | a session in which a fork and a named subagent have each first written one distinct marker file |
| ⚠️ |  | `AGT-4c` | **Session memory:** a fork asked to quote the project memory token quotes it; a named subagent's own `read_child` transcript does not. **Drive the fork half first:** the negative half is unverified as product behaviour — the child's memory layers are intersected against a parent ceiling, and a ceiling narrows without necessarily emptying, so a named subagent that also quotes the token is a finding about this row's claim before it is a product defect | `NESSY_CDP_MEMORY_MARKER` = a token ALREADY present in this project's MEMORY.md; the harness must not append memory itself |
| ⬜ |  | `AGT-5` | **The observability block is LIFTED (R53c, 2026-08-08): `getPiHarnessRuntimeSnapshot().childState.children[]` now carries `effort` and `model`,** projected from the child record's restrictions — so the case scores off the same field the agent-panel row renders, and no longer has to read a sibling session. **Override half:** a named subagent declaring `effort: high` spawns and COMPLETES under a session pinned to `low`, and ITS OWN row in `childState.children` reads `effort: 'high'` — the pre-`aebf72db` clamp made it the parent's `low`. **Reader half:** a second subagent with NO `effort` key spawns at session `low` (its row reads `low`), then the session is raised mid-conversation and the NEXT spawn's row reads the NEW level — an effort captured at spawner-configuration time would still read `low`. **Panel half (new, and the one the user actually sees):** the agent panel row for each spawn shows that same level next to its status (`agent-panel-effort-<agentId>`), with the model beside it. The value is the APPLIED level, clamped to the child's model — `statusline.md` reports the configured one, and the divergence is deliberate (see `PiHarnessChildSummaryV1.effort`), so a fixture on a model that cannot run the declared level will honestly read lower. `getSessions()` (children are `hidden` but unfiltered, named `<agent>: <task>`) remains a valid cross-check; the dev log still never prints it | `~/.nessy-agent/agents/cdp-high-effort.md` (`effort: high`) and `~/.nessy-agent/agents/cdp-inherit-effort.md` (no `effort` key); `NESSY_CDP_HIGH_EFFORT_SUBAGENT=cdp-high-effort NESSY_CDP_INHERIT_EFFORT_SUBAGENT=cdp-inherit-effort` |
| ⬜ |  | `AGT-9` | **A fork cannot fork.** Inside a fork started with `/subtask`, ask it to start a fork of its own: it must REFUSE with a message saying this session is already a conversation fork and suggesting a named subagent — even in a fresh session with the full default depth budget of 3 available, because the rule is unconditional and is checked before the depth check. `SAD-1`/`SAD-2` cover the named-subagent depth ladder and `AGT-1`..`AGT-3` the fork lifecycle; this differently-worded refusal is the one a user hits first | a fresh session, one `/subtask`, then a `/subtask` from inside it |
| ⬜ |  | `AGT-10` | **A child cannot be more permissive than its parent.** Give a named subagent a definition declaring `permissionMode: bypassPermissions`, invoke it from a session sitting in `default` mode, and confirm the CHILD still reaches a human permission prompt for a write. Then re-run with the parent in `bypassPermissions` and confirm the child inherits it (the control). `PRM-F1`/`PRM-N2*` cover the main session's authorities; nothing drives the parent-bound clamp, where a wrong position is a silent widening | `~/.nessy-agent/agents/cdp-bypass.md` with `permissionMode: bypassPermissions` and a body that writes a file |
| ⬜ |  | `AGT-11` | **A bare `/subtask` reports its usage instead of talking to the model.** Type `/subtask` with no task and press Enter: the session prints the usage line (`Usage: /subtask <task>. The fork inherits this conversation…`) and starts NOTHING — no fork row, no turn. `/compact` and `/subtask` once SHIPPED reaching the model as prose, so this is a regression that has already happened once | — |

**AGT-2, the follow-up half: PASSES.** 2026-07-30, dev instance on 9224.
`controlPiHarnessRuntime({action:'steer_child'})` returned `{ok:true}` against a
`running` fork, and the fork ended its 18,973-character answer with the probe's
marker on its own line. The delivery path — `ChildSessionService.steer` →
`runtime.steer` → `getManaged(childSessionKey)` → `agent.redirect()` →
`{type:'steer'}` → the child's Pi subprocess — is intact end to end. The earlier
"could not confirm" was a measurement that never reached the assertion, not a
lost message.

**AGT-3 LIVE PASS 2026-07-31, fresh dev instance on 9224** — all seven checks
green in 18.0 s: the fork registered, the card rendered in the parent and named
the fork, Allow clicked (the viewport fix picked the visible card — the hidden
measuring copy is plainly visible in the observations at a different height),
the card and `pendingPermissions` cleared together, and the fork continued.

**History of the fix:**
`0583333c` publishes a registered child's prompt on the parent snapshot (with
`agentId`) and routes a parent-addressed answer to the child; `29e0aa77` makes a
refused follow-up visible. The executor's reproduction also NARROWED the finding:
the permission card already rendered in the parent and named the fork on the
pre-fix bundle — the blindness was the snapshot/API, not the display. Unit + wire
green, six mutation probes red on the right side. The live after needs an app
relaunch (main-process fix; the dev bundle on disk carries it) — run
`bun run scripts/cdp/cases/agt-3.ts` on the next instance. The harness's own
Allow-click defect (first `querySelector` match is the invisible measuring copy;
a non-fork positive control missed identically) is fixed in
`scripts/cdp/lib/viewport.ts`: `elementRect` now takes the first VISIBLE match.

**The original finding, kept for the record — AGT-3 as measured 2026-07-30:** A
fork that needs any permission hangs forever with no surface. Measured on the
same rig: a fork reached a `Write`, and ten minutes later its transcript was
frozen at 14 messages / 2,446 assistant characters with `isProcessing: true`
while the parent's `childState` still said `running`. The parent snapshot's
`pendingPermissions` was `[]`; the CHILD's snapshot held
`{type: 'file_write'}`. Nothing the user is looking at can show or answer it, so
the row sits at `running` and the fork never speaks again — which is
indistinguishable from a follow-up that vanished.

This is the residual already recorded against `pendingPermissions`
(`sub-agents.md:1043`: the runtime snapshot filters by exact session id, so a
fork's prompt is absent from the parent). It was filed as a gap; it is in fact a
hang, and forks that write files are the common case rather than the exotic one.

**A second, smaller defect found alongside it:** `useAgentPanel.followUp` did
`.catch(() => null)` and never read `ok`, so `child-session-not-active`,
`child-session-not-running` and `pi-child-service-unavailable` all rendered as a
cleared input box. Both that swallow and the resurrected-row bug are fixed in the
current `useAgentPanel.ts` (a `{ok,reason}` control result, and a dismissed-set
that survives a children invalidation), which is what `AGT-2` re-proves.

Set `sizeGuideline: "small"` and the threshold moves to 5 with the model advised to aim smaller, while a prompt that genuinely needs more agents still runs. `WF-1`..`WF-6` cover `disableWorkflows` only; the other two documented workflow settings have no row | a workflow that fans out past 25 agents; the two config values in `~/.nessy-agent/config.json` |

Do not assert the negative: the resolver enforces generation and live-turn, not turn identity, so a later turn in the same session also resolves — that is the scenario, not an exclusive constraint | shares `SKR-1`'s skill |

That button exists precisely because a freshly installed skill may not be in the runtime yet, i.e. the failure mode is known and unmeasured | — |

The wrong body silently winning is invisible without this check | one global skill and one plugin skill sharing a name, each carrying a distinct marker |
| ⬜ | 🔴 | `SKL-P1` | **The `paths` half of the 1.25.0 promise, on an artifact that had to be authored because none exists.** The independent audit of the signed 1.25.0 searched `anthropics/skills`, `anthropics/claude-code`, `anthropics/claude-plugins-official`, `obra/superpowers`, `mattpocock/skills` and `duckdb/duckdb-skills` and found **no public `SKILL.md` declaring `paths:` anywhere** — so both halves below were untested on a real install. **(a) METADATA, automated** (`scripts/cdp/cases/skl-p1.ts`): after installing `cdp-paths-probe` through the app's own marketplace RPC, `<config>/plugins/installed.json` must carry, for `cdp-paths-gated`, `paths` with BOTH declared patterns and `allowedTools` with ALL FOUR entries — the third being `Agent(cdp-paths-probe:alpha, cdp-paths-probe:beta)`, one comma inside one parenthesized specifier, the exact shape that made `claude-security`'s twenty-entry list arrive as no list at all. Assert the whole array, not the presence of the comma entry: the pre-fix loss was total. `cdp-paths-control` must carry the same `allowedTools` and NO `paths`. **(b) ACTIVATION, operator-run.** `skills.md`, the `paths` row: *"When set, Claude loads the skill automatically only when working with files matching the patterns."* The gate is checked on the MODEL origin only (`pathGatedSkillIsInScope`, called at the `origin === 'model'` branch), so a `/`-menu invocation is never gated and proves nothing. On a **Pi or Claude-runtime** session opened on `<root>/project`: (1) with nothing read yet, a prompt that should auto-load the gated skill must NOT produce its body marker, while the SAME prompt for `cdp-paths-control` DOES produce its marker — that control is the whole reason the negative is readable; (2) after the model reads `db/schema.sql` with a tool, the gated skill's marker DOES appear; (3) reading `notes.txt` instead must not open the gate. Markers are minted per run into `<root>/markers.json`; a model cannot fake one. **On the Codex runtime the gate is inert by design** (`deliversPathScopedActivations` is false there, so a `paths` skill is always eligible) — record that, never score it as a failure. | `bun run scripts/cdp/fixtures/paths-skill.ts` (root `NESSY_CDP_SKP_ROOT`, default `~/nessy-cdp-skp`), then start the app with `NESSY_CONFIG_DIR=<root>/config`, `marketplace.addRegistry({source: '<root>/market'})` and `marketplace.install({source:{kind:'listing', marketplaceId, listingId: '<marketplaceId>:cdp-paths-probe', listingKind:'plugins'}})`, and open `<root>/project` as the session's project. **The bundle also declares `Agent(a, b)` in an agent's `tools`, which is what killed every Pi turn on the signed 1.25.0 (audit DEFECT-3) — on a build without that fix the first Pi turn dies with `Unavailable runtime agent tool(s)`, which is the pre-fix expectation, not a broken fixture** |

| ⬜ |  | `BG-1` | Bash with `timeout: 3000` on a ~8 s command that does NOT start with `sleep` (e.g. `python3 -c "import time; time.sleep(8)"`) → the tool result reads verbatim `Command did not complete within its 3s timeout and was moved to the background` plus `shell_id:`, `output_file:` and the session-cwd sentence, and the file at `output_file` keeps growing and holds the command's output after it ends. **Negative control in the same case:** a `sleep`-prefixed command is KILLED, never backgrounded — so the obvious `sleep 8` fixture proves the opposite of the row | keep the fixture's output small: the log store front-trims above a cap, so a huge output is not a fair test of "holds the output" |
| ⬜ |  | `BG-2` | Explore-mode parser blocks `for` loops with the explanatory reply (documented gating) | — |
| ⬜ |  | `BG-3` | A main-conversation MCP call still running past the auto-background threshold DETACHES: the model gets a `task-mcp-…` id immediately and the turn ends, then the settled result arrives as a NEW turn in the transcript. Expect the notification only AFTER the turn goes idle — it is queued while the session is processing, so watching for it mid-turn reads as a failure | an MCP server with a tool that sleeps ~20 s (extend `~/.nessy-agent/mcp/elicit-server.mjs` or add a second stdio script), and the app relaunched with `NESSY_MCP_AUTO_BACKGROUND_MS=5000` — the 120 s default is longer than any live run will wait. Shares its server with `ELI-5` |
| ⬜ |  | `BG-4` | `NESSY_DISABLE_BACKGROUND_TASKS=1`: plain timeout kill; `bash_background` gone from surface | app relaunched with the env |
| ⬜ |  | `BG-5` | **The explicit background path, which `BG-1` never touches.** Ask for a long command with `run_in_background: true` (not a timeout transition): a chip appears in the composer's task row showing the shell id and a live elapsed timer, and clicking its kill button actually stops the process — the chip disappears and the shell no longer runs (`ps` confirms) | a command that runs long enough to be killed mid-flight |
| ⬜ |  | `BG-6` | **The only way a user watches a backgrounded command.** Click a running-shell chip in the composer row and choose the terminal-output action: the overlay opens and shows THAT shell's live output, updating as it grows. A broken overlay ships silently while `BG-1`'s `output_file` assertion still passes | continues from `BG-5` |
| ⬜ |  | `TSK-1` | In a **Pi-backed** session (`TaskCreate`/`TaskUpdate`/`TaskList` are `visibility: 'pi-only'`, so a Claude-runtime or Codex session gets only the legacy TodoWrite checklist, which has no ids), a live turn creating tasks renders the checklist on `Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+T`; at most five rows show with an overflow count, and the same chord on an empty list is a visible no-op. **The panel renders LABELS only** — read the harness-assigned `task-001`-shaped id from the `TaskCreate` tool-result card (`{ task: { id, subject } }`) or from `~/.nessy-agent/tasks/session-<first 8>/task-001/task.json`, NEVER from the panel DOM, where the id exists only as a React key | a Pi session (Nessy Gateway Pi card), not a `claude-runtime` or `codex-runtime` one |

The parent `tasks/session-<8>/` may pre-exist from an earlier allowed create — assert the per-task directory, not the root |

The background-work view is a SEPARATE surface — the composer's task chip row, which shows running shells and forks and never the checklist. **Nessy ships no `/tasks` command**; the doc's name for that view survives here only in code comments, so typing it proves nothing | — |
| ⬜ |  | `TSK-4` | **A shared task list, the reason a task root exists at all.** Launch two sessions with the same `NESSY_TASK_LIST_ID=shared-probe`, create a task in one, and confirm the OTHER session's `Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+T` checklist shows it. `TSK-3` proves only the per-session default naming; the sharing case the design exists for has no row | two Pi sessions started with the env set on the app process |
